  front matter is noise.
Content note: The Latin tract answers sixteen articles condemned as Wyclif's (the first: that the
  pope has no plenitude of power in matters of jurisdiction). Thomson attributes it to Hus on
  internal evidence; he says there is no direct manuscript evidence for the attribution.

INTRODUCTION. 


Eighty years have gone by since Palacky’s »Die Vorlaufer 
des Hussitenthums« made its appearance under another name 
than that of its real author. Since that time, 1846, there has 
been increasing interest manifested in those prezReformation 
movements commonly spoken of as Wycliffism and Hussitism. 
This interest has, in keeping with the genius of the age, been 
directed along the lines of a critical and historical evaluation of 
the factors in the growth of the respective movements, cultural, 
racial, religious and theological. The investigations of scores of 
relatively modern scholars have at times assumed.the propor: 
tions of a bitter controversy. And indeed it has been only naz 
tural that questions touching so many features and elements of 
modern Western European life at their rise, and involving so 
many possible points of divergence should project themselves 
into the present in a similarly divergent manner. For the histor: 
ian who does not approach the study of his period parti pris 
is as rare as he would be uninteresting. In the case of the Hus: 
site movement, and more particulary of Hus himself, the con- 
troversies incident to the formulation of a definitive judgment 
of his work and place in religious history have been very sharp, 
and often more bitter than intelligible. The questions thus 
raised and debated are still by no means settled — for the very 
simple reason that we have not as yet all the documents and 
writings before us, either of Wyclif, whose imitator and pla: 
giaristic disciple one party asserts Hus to have been, or of the 
pre-Hussite Czech religious leaders, of whom the. other party 
assert Hus to have been the continuator — nor indeed have we 
by any means all of Hus' own writings in any usable form. 
But these lacunae in our knowledge are being filled as rapidly 
as is humanly possible by the labours of the relatively few 


VI INTRODUCTION. 


serious students who are working on the period. The labour 
and time entailed in editing a tractate of any considerable size, 
written in late mediaeval Latin, constitute a deterrent of no negli- 
gible proportions. But the tractate lying before us, Hus’ Trac 
tatus Responsivus, is so important in its bearing on Hus’ 
methods of composition in the midst of the busy last few years 
of his life, that, it is hoped, it will be of definite use to those 
interested in the Reformer and his period. If such turns out 
to be the case, the time and labour spent will have been amply 
repaid. 

The editor is deeply indebted to Prof. Vaclav Novotny, 
Professor of Czech History in the Charles University in Prague, 
for hearty encouragement and invaluable assistance, given une 
sparingly in the midst of his own absorbing work. Prof. No: 
votny secured the loan of the manuscript from the Metropo- 
litan Chapter Library, and has also gone over the entire text 
with me, giving invaluable suggestions which could have been 
obtained from no one else. Needless to say, any mistakes or 
bits of carelessness that are almost sure to turn up, are in every 
case the fault of the editor alone. He can only hope that the 
cause that is so near Prof. Novotny's heart, a fair verdict of 
Hus at the bar of History, may not be prejudiced by the de: 
ficiencies in the editing of the present tractate. To Dr. Podlaha, 
the librarian of the Metropolitan Chapter Library, the editor's 
thanks are due for the protracted loan of the Ms. 


The Manuscript. 


The tractate exists, so far as we know, in a unique manue 
script, Cod. C. 116 of the Metropolitan Chapter Library in 
Prague. The codex has already been partially described by Dr. 
Vaclav Flajshans in the Véstník Ceské Akademie, Roénik XI, 
st. 594—6. (Annual of the Czech Academy, 1902, pp. 594—6) 
who was the first to attribute this tractate to Hus. Dr. Pos 
dlaha's Soupis Rukopisà Metropolitni Kapitoly, I, p. 515—14 
has a detailed description of the whole codex, which contains 
works or copies of earlier works written largely in the early 
years of the 15th century by many different scribes, on paper. 
The following description ís based on that of Dr. Podlaha, to 
which some necessary additions and corrections have been made.

It will be noticed that Hus' tractate is the most consider: 
able in the codex. It is, furthermore, quite evident, from an 
examination of the writings throughout the codex, that it is the 
only one by this scribe. Two very interesting errors could easily 
lead to the conclusion that this is the original, taken from dict 


INTRODUCTION. IX 


ation. In two places the scribe has made a suggestive mistake 
in the same word — f 292^ omnium for hominum, and 
f 300* omnibus for hominibus, a rather natural mistake to 
make in view of the similarity of pronunciation, but less likely to 
be expected if the scribe were copying from another manuscript, 
inasmuch as in the first case the content is clear, and in the 
second the reference is to a familiar section in Paul's first letter 
to the Corinthians. There are, however, numerous other scribal 
errors which would be more essily explained on the theory 
that this is a copy of the original. The editor admits hesitancy 
in deciding between these two alternatives. In any case its near- 
ness to the author is indisputable. We have also, as is quite 
commonly the case in contemporary bookmaking, details which 
tell us the number of pages in the scribe's booklets. At the 
bottom of f 238° we find written primus, which evidently, in: 
asmuch as the first words of the next page are Et tunc, was 
meant to indicate the end of his first booklet, i. e. ff 227 — 238 
inclusive, making twelve pages. Unfolded that would make six 
single sheets, commonly known as a "sextern". Twelve pages 
further on, f 250°, we find in the same relative place, at the 
lower right hand corner, the words Antequam veniremus, 
with which the following page, f 251*, begins. This same thing 
occurs at the end of every sextern; f 262°, Ubi tamen; 274°, 
et adorant super tecta etc.; f 286° interrogando; 
298" Amplius. Twelve more pages would bring us to £ 310°, 
(f£ 506^ — 510? are vacant) which shows the extent of the scribe's 
work — seven complete sexterns of 12 pages each, 84 pages 
from f 227* to £310° inclusive. The pagination as we have it 
is, of course, of a much later date. It was first done in pencil 
and has since been gone over with a numbering machine. As 
Dr. Flajáhans pointed out in his brief notice of this manuscript, 
there are a few marginal corrections in the scribe's own hand, 
and there are also notes in a later hand, perhaps those of the 
previous owner, Pontanus of Breitenbach, whose ex libris, so 
to speak, is on the first page. (v. supra). The title Johannis 
Hussii Tractatus Responsivus is probably in the same 
hand as the later marginal annotations throughout the text. 
These marginalia have been given in the footnotes, and when: 
ever they are clearly in a later hand it has been so specified — manu 
posteriori. The scribe had begun to go through the tractate, 


x INTRODUCTION. 


punctuating, underlining and rubricating in red, but he got only 
as far as f 262°, that is, through the third sextern, from which 
point the larger capitals are left our altogether or a small letter 
put in their place. The names of authorities, Augustine, Gre: 
gory, Jerome etc. and books of the Bible are generally under: 
lined in red or black according as they occur before or after 
f 262”. The number of lines on a page varies from 32 to 39, 
but the average is probably nearer 35. From f 287* to the end 
the pages are ruled across the top and bottom. 

The writing is fairly clear and is uniform throughout. The 
abbreviations are of the usual sort to be found in early 15 
century writings on theological subjects. There have been oce 
casional abbreviations that have caused some trouble, but Prof. 
Novotny has very kindly helped me in cases where his greater 
familiarity with the script of this period made my difficulties 
disappear. Minor difficulties have been straightened out by 
comparing the reference in question to the passage in Scripture 
or the Church father or Canon quoted. 

The author has incorporated into his tractate the “posicio” 
of Jacobellus de Misa (Jakoubek ze Stribra), defending articles 
32 and 33 of the 45 articles of Wyclif condemned by the Town 
Hall Council on July 16, 1412. His "posicio" as well as that 
of Epinge were delivered after the three *actus" of Hus, printed 
in Opera I, 111 sqq. I have had a copy of the Vienna Nat. 
Lib. Ms. 4518, ff 44*—52° to compare, and though no attempt 
has been made to give a complete collation of the Vienna Ms., 
yet substantial differences have been noted. The entire “‘posicio”’ 
of Magister Frederick Epinge, in defense of article 11 of the 45 
condemned articles is incorporated into our tractate, ff 279*— 
291°. I have used a copy of the Vienna Nat. Library Ms. 4519, 
ff 119^—155*. The Vienna manuscript is not so abbreviated in 
its references to Canon Law, and it has thus been possible to 
trace quotations otherwise well disguised by an indication such 
as ex. de. off. or, Extravagans de officio iudicis 
ordinarii, or de off. de. le. c. fi. li. 6, when the meaning 
is de officio delegati, capitulo finali, libro 6°. With 
several exceptions, pointed out in the footnotes, I have been 
able to trace all the references made to Canon Law and the 
fathers. In the case of Epinge’s "posicio" the variata would be 
inconsequential. The copy in our tractate is extraordinarily 


INTRODUCTION. XI 


faithful, pointing to its nearness to the source. It is indeed pos 
sible that Hus dictated from Epinge's own copy. Thus we hawe 
before us something unique in Hus literature, a tractate which 
is really three separate tractates, yet unified by the impetus of 
a common cause and the guidance of a dominant character. None 
of the three has hitherto been printed in its original form*. 


Authorship and Date. 


As we have seen above the title Johannis Hussii Trace 
tatus Responsivus is in a later hand. Thus we have no 
direct Ms. evidence for assigning the tract to Hus. It is further: 
more almost entirely free from references to Hus' polemics, 
or to events of his life to which he often refers in his other 
writings, as e. g. in "De Ecclesia", Contra Palec", Contra Octo 
Doctores", etc. But even the most casual perusal of the con: 
tents shows that with these exceptions this tractate is abso: 
lutely identical in tone and manner of argument with the other 
tractates that came from his pen at this period, 1412— 1414. 

Article I, treating the "plenitudo potestatis" of the Roman 
pontiff and the power of the keys is argued in much the same 
way as in Chapters IV, VI, X and XIII of Hus’ “De Ecclesia" 
which as we shall see is later by several months. There are here 
several very considerable sections taken from Wyclif’s “De Po 
testate Pape", worked into the argument in the way we know 
Hus to have used fairly extensively. 

Article II. The author declares that his detractors have made 
accusations that they have not proved. His denial of their char- 
ges, f 238* is almost the same as the denial of a similar charge 
which we have in Hus’ *Ad Scripta M. Stephani Palec" (Op. I, 
256* sq.). It is worth noting, in passing, that the tone in our 
tractate is much more tempered and less bitter. It is, as we shall 
see, considerably earlier. Later polemics made recriminations and 
countererecriminations much more acute. 

Article IV (ff 242^ —254*) is almost entirely the Posicio 
M. Jacobelli de Misa quam fecit pro articulo con 
dempnato, videlicet, Ditare clerum est contra re- 
gulam Cristi Iesu. (Novotny, M. Jan Hus, 1, 2, p. 142.) 
I say "almost entirely" because our author has more than two 


* vide infra concerning partial Czech translation. 


XII | INTRODUCTION. 


pages (ff 242^ —245") of his own matter before beginning to 
quote Jacobellus' **posicio", and then it is quoted with a number 
of minor variants, omissions and additions. The whole first 
page as Jacobellus left it is here condensed into a few lines, 
and our author adds a whole page of his own at the end, 
giving a fourfold classification of the clergy according to their 
origin. In the body of the article the modifications made are 
quite considerable, particularly in the early part. The assertion 
of Dr. Sedlák* that this '"posicio" was completely copied (üplné 
opsány) is, therefore, not strictly, though it is practically, true. 
Dr. Sedlák has suggested that this ''posicio" was a work of 
joint authorship (Studie a Texty, II, 196), done by Jacobellus 
and Hus, and the few liberties that our author takes with it 
would seem to bear out that theory. 

Article V (ff 254*— 262^) brings us additional evidence of 
Hus' authorship. The whole argument is strikingly similar to 
that of the “Tractatus adversus Indulgencias Papales". There 
are even verbal parallels with his De Sex Erroribus", and argue 
ments and citations which he uses in the "De Ecclesia" and 
other later works. 

Article VI (ff 262° —292*), treating excommunication, brings 
us, however the most interesting as well as conclusive proof 
that Hus was the author of the work lying before us. The 
author begins with a reference to the excommunication of Hus, 
in the third person, a not uncommon practise of Hus, used 
often in the “De Ecclesia’, and continues the argument through 
some 17 pages (ff 262° —279°). This section seems to be an 
advance and enlargement of Hus’ position in his Commentary 
on the Lombard’s IV Sententiarum (ed. Flajshans, pp. 608— 14). 
But the author of our tractate has restricted himself largely to 
cases of excommunication recorded in the Scriptures, developing 
the Scripture teaching along the line of the earlier Commentary. 
Then on f 279° he appends in toto the “posicio” of Magister 
Frederick Epinge, defending Article 11 of the 45 condemned 
articles of Wyclif (cf supra, Jacobellus, art 32, 55). Epinge was 
a bachelor of Canon Law, and as might be expected, his proofs 
for the conclusions he reaches are taken mostly from Canon Law. 
But a comparison with Hus’ discussion of excommunication in 


* Dr. Jan Sedlak, late Prof. of Theology in Brno, Studie a Texty k Zivoto- 
pisu Husovu, II, 196 (1914) [Studies and Texts for a Biography of Hus). 


INTRODUCTION. XIII 


his Commentary on the Lombard reveals the suggestive fact that 
all of Hus' references to Canon Law are reproduced (with addit: 
ions, of course) in Epinge's “‘posicio”. It seems highly probable 
that we have here another example of Hus' cooperation with his 
colleagues, which Dr. Sedlák suggested in the case of Jacobellus 
cited above, that Epinge had either developed and expanded 
notes of Hus' lectures on the Lombard, or that Hus suggested to 
him the line of argument and the sort of proofs to adduce. It is 
certain that Hus thought highly of Epinge. To our knowledge 
he does not speak more highly of the Christian character of any 
of his associates than he does of Epinge — ‘‘a man redoubtable 
as well as humble, whose memory, I hope, is now held in reve 
rence” (f 279*), and in the “De Ecclesia" — "that good Christian 
of holy memory, that great zealot of Christ's law" Op. I, 249*. 
The “‘posicio” of Epinge occupies twelve leaves, ff 279* —291^*, 1. e. 
about the same length as that of Jacobellus. It is put in here, says 
the author, for the benefit of those who are unable to procure 
a copy for themselves — specialiter illis qui copiam 
librorum multorum ad istas sentencias conquiren- 
dum habere non possunt, adducatur posicio M. Ffre- 
derici^Epingi Baccalaurii iuris canonici, iam | dies 
functi, viri utique timorati ac humilis, cuius, ut 
spero, memoria in benediccione est. (£279*). In Hus 
"De Ecclesia" (c.June 1415) at the point where he refers the 
reader to the “‘posicio” of Epinge, we find our strongest proof 
for Hus’ authorship of our tractate. Hec breviter de excom: 
municacione, de qua fecit posicionem solennem 
sancte memorie pius christianus et magnus zelator 
et scrutator legis Christi, M. Fredericus Epinge, 
Bacc. Iuris Canonici, tractans primum articulum: 
Nullus prelatus debetaliquem excommunicare, nisi 
prius sciat ipsum esse excommunicatum a deo, de 
qua etiam alibi scripsi. The alibi is, it seems clear, our 
tractate. The syntactical antecedent of de qua alibi scripsi 
is patently posicionem. If it be objected that excommuni 
cacio is also feminine, and grammatically as legitimate an ante: 
cedent as posicio there is yet no real objection to accepting the 
contention that Hus is referring to our tractate, inasmuch as he 
has nowhere else, aside from our tractate, even as considerable 
and thorough a treatment of excommunication as he has already 


XIV INTRODUCTION. 


given in Chapter XXII of “De Ecclesia". He surely would not 
refer his readers to a briefer and less convincing treatment such 
as is found in "De Sex Erroribus". As if to make our contention 
more sure, a perusal of the Chapter on Excommunication in the 
"De Sex Erroribus" shows that Hus there has but repeated argu 
ments which he himself had outlined at least three years previ- 
ously (“Super IV Sent.") and had recast and reedited with his 
colleague Epinge in the middle of the year 1412, and had gone 
over some months later, after Epinge's death, in the tractate we 
have before us. The more we study the respective writings the 
closer we see them to be bound together by the genius of one 
directing mind. 

Article VII (f. 292*—306*) dealing with the pope's power 
to grant indulgences, though in part repeating matter already 
treated in the fifth article, goes on to a consideration of the 
office of the pope. There is a good deal in this section taken 
from Wyclf's “De Potestate Pape", and a good deal that is 
repeated, sometimes more, sometimes less, completely in “Contra 
Palec". 

We may conveniently sum up our reasons for regarding Hus 
as the author of our tractate thus: 

1. General tone and content distinctly suggestive of Hus. 

2. Manner of use of Wyclif’s tractates corresponding to his 
use in many other works. 

5. Striking identity in estimate of Epinge's character and 
ability in our treatise and in “De Ecclesia," cap. XXII. 

4. The phrase in the latter work, "concerning which I have 
written elsewhere", the “which” undoubtedly referring to 
the “posicio solemnis" of Epinge. Nothing else but our 
tractate could satisfy the conditions of this reference. 

5. Verbal parallels with many other of Hus' works, which, 
in view of their evident spontaneity, could hardly indi 
cate other than an identity of authorship. 

6. The title in a later hand is also of no inconsiderable 
weight. 


Date. 


Granted that Hus is the author of this Tractatus Respon 
sivus, when was it written? It is evident that it raises questions 
concerning the polemics of a period of his life which, until the 


INTRODUCTION. XM 


discovery of the work, did not exist." Beyond the seven arti- 
cles discussed here, we do not know as yet what these 16 arti- 
cles were that were circulated in the towns outside Prague by 
his enemies. It is possible that a copy of these articles will yet 
appear in some monastic library or even in a private collection 
— jt is certainly to be hoped that they will so appear. But 
until they do we must content ourselves with trying to fix the 
circumstances that occasioned the work and the date of its 
composition. 

It has already been suggested that this is either the original 
written at Hus' dictation, or a copy of the original. If that be so, 
we must take into account Hus' own movements during the period 
in which it is possible for him to have written the tractate. That 
period, according to the most reasonable arrangement of the 
evidence at hand, is restricted to the months of September and 
October, 1412. The series of debates of which Jacobellus's and 
Epinge's "posiciones" were part took place almost surely from 
the middle to the end of August, 1412. (Novotny, M. Jan Hus 
[1919], I, 2, 140—142; Sedlák, M. Jan Hus [1915], 248—252.) 
Hus's third "actus" is dated the early part of August, and we 
know the other two partisans to have followed him in that 
order, i. e. Jacobellus and then Epinge. In Hus's three "actus" 
he defended respectively articles 15 and 14; 16; 18, 15, and 4 
of the condemned articles of Wyclif.** My reconstruction of the 
circumstances would be this: in the course of these public 
disputations he made statements which his enemies eagerly 
seized upon as conclusive evidence of his heterodoxy, and 
having decided to publish throughout the country the 45 con 
demned articles, as a warning and preventive of the further 
spread of the heresies they taught, added the statements, to 
the number of 16, which they ascribed to Hus, and labelled 
them erroneous, schismatic or heretical as the case might be or 
seem to them to be. That this is a valid hypothesis several 
interesting facts go to prove. For six of the seven articles which

had not made, his opponents spread throughout the country 
(not in Prague) as the teaching of Hus and the reform party. 
It was the sort of statement that would strike the imagination 
of people in rural parishes, who would, in the natural course 
of events, be much attached to their local priests. History 
presents us many examples of this kind of verbal perversion 
for the sake of creating prejudices for or against men or ideas. 

This declaration of Hus' opponents, containing these 16 
‘articles, in the natural course of publication, came to the mon: 
astery of Rokycany, where, we must suppose, there was some 
one who was sympathetic with the Hus party. (Novotny, I, 2, 
p. 162, n. 1.) This friend, it would seem, from Hus's words — 
Sedecim autem articuli sunt mihi oblati, et de mo- 
nasterio Rokyczanensi portati ... had brought or sent 
this bill of illzhealth to Hus, for him to answer. For all this 
to transpire we must allow some time. Hus could hardly have 
— begun work on his reply before the early part of September, 
allowing a fortnight after Jacobellus's “posicio” from which 
Article IV of our tractate was taken. The time necessary for 
the composition of this “bill of errors" might be short, but its 
publication throughout the country, and sending back to Prague 
could easily consume two weeks or more. We thus have a tere 
minus a quo. But a work of 80 folio pages is not composed 
and written overnight. And the terminus ad quem seems easier 
to fix. Hus left Prague early in the month of October, 1412. 
(Novotny, I, 2, p. 182; Sedlák, 265; Lützow, 155) On leaving 
Prague, he left off dictating this reply to his detractors. This 


comes 


XVIII INTRODUCTION. 


case seems very much like that of Nicholas Hereford, Wyclif’s 
coeworker, who had to flee from England to France on short 
notice, leaving the translation of the Old Testament unfinished, 
in the middle of the apocryphal book of Baruch. The manus: 
cript is still extant, bearing witness to the suddenness of his 
flight*. Our tractate is a parallel case. Hus probably left off 
dictating one afternoon early in October, and, being apprised 
of the increasing acuteness of the situation in the city, due to the 
aggravation of the excommunication, decided to leave Prague 
immediately, and never got back to the task of finishing this 
reply. His exile lasted so long that the particular object for 


which he had intended this reply seemed to have lost its 


urgency, and he incorporated the material which he had here 
got together, under the influence of Wyclif's two tractates, "De 
Ecclesia" and ‘De Potestate Pape", into another better organ: 
ized and more definitive work, his own De Ecclesia. Dr. Sedlák 
and Prof. Novotny have already largely suggested this arrange 
ment, though neither has cared to be so explicit in dating 
it thus, beginning and end, from the first week in September, 
to the first week in October 1412. This dating seems to satisfy 
all the conditions presented by the evidence that we have and 
the probabilities of the case where we have no evidence. 


Contents of the Tractate. 


Something has already been said about the contents and 
the general purposes of the tractate, but a consecutive summary 
will show more clearly the line of argument. 

Certain articles have been unreasonably condemned by cer: 
tain men, and thus condemned have been distributed among 
the priests in country parishes with instructions that they be 
publicly read, with the condemnation it is alleged they deserve. 
Sixteen of these articles have been brought to Hus from the 
monastery of Rokycany. He begins to treat them in order. 

The first one reads: It is not necessary to believe that the 
supreme pontiff has tulness of power in those things that pertain 
to his jurisdiction, and if he is living in mortal sin, he does 
not have the keys of loosing and binding. 

Hus contends that there is one supreme pontiff, after the 


* Facsimile in Dr. Workman's “John Wyclif” (Oxford, 1926), II, 148. 


INTRODUCTION. XIX 


order of Melchisedech, who truly has the fulness of power in 
all things over the whole church. He is the supreme pontiff 
of all individual churches, at Prague as well as at Rome. And 
if we refer to the Roman pontiff, Peter, who by his example and 
teaching profited the church as Paul and the other apostles, we 
must recognise that they had fulness of power within their juris: 
dictions, as well as Peter in his. But this power or authority Christ * 
alone gives to His true followers (1) who living in grace have 
the right to do meritoriously works of mercy; (2) none living 
in mortal sin can of himself acceptably to God and profitably 
do these works; (5) the Scribes and Pharisees in the time of 
Christ had no such power; (4) no simoniacal, immoral, hypo: 
critical or false priest or prelate today can meritoriously and 
truly convert sinners, a power granted only to adopted sons 
of God; (5) nor can such false priests meritoriously bind or 
loose or administer the other sacraments; (6) and therefore no 
modern supreme pontiff, known as the pope, if he be living 
in sin, has any such fulness of power, nor can he be of any 
spiritual benefit to the church, unless it be as in the case of 
Judas Iscariot, who, in bringing on himself damnation, was of 
profit to the church; (7) such power in a Christian is lost by 
mortal sin. Most of the officials in the church lack this power, 
because they have in the past abused it, and God can and does 
take power or licence from a man as punishment for sin. No 
heretic, says Augustine, can have any sort of power, and im 
iacs and adulterers are certainly heretics. 

But as to a power of another sort, that conferred on the 
priest by the sacrament of ordination, we may as well admit * 
our ignorance and rely on God's grace to provide for our sacraz 
mental needs. In no case, however, should a Christian spurn 
the sacraments, for God gives through them His heavenly gifts 
to His children. 

Human election of itself cannot give coactive power to any 
man; indeed human electors might choose Antichrist as Christ’s 
vicar. God is not bound by the fallibility or selfishness of human 
judgment, and is not forced to confer the gifts of His grace on 
whomsoever man may choose. But anyone who is not fitted for 
it, and takes upon himself the priestly office, or administers the 
sacrament of the Lord's Supper, should realize that he ‘eats and 
drinks judgment to himself". For there are those who, living in 


XX INTRODUCTION. 


mortal sin without grace, can nevertheless preach useful truth 
to others. And thus the Scribes and Pharisees can have the gift 
of the Holy Spirit from God, the office ot the power of ordin: 
ation to the edification, not of themselves, but of others. But 
the faithful must judge whether God has given those priests 
living sinful lives the gift of profitting the church. Hus contends 
however that there is a point beyond which a priest or a prelate 
cannot go in sin and be considered profitable to the church. 
(f 254»). 

But there is still another sort of power, that of ruling secu: 
larly, that was never given by Christ or His apostles, but rather 
by Constantine. Secular rule is lawful for secular princes and 
kings, but not for priests or the clergy in general. Nor is any 
Christian bound to obey laws that are not founded on Scrip: 
ture, but are the additions of later popes and prelates. On the 
contrary, there is a low form of power allowed the prince of 
this world against which all the faithful must struggle. 

The second article: If a priest is in mortal sin, he cannot 
absolve a parishioner who is contrite and confesses his sins. 
But this is a statement, says Hus, that these detractors have 
not proved him to have made. But if we define our term “to 
be able" as “to be able meritoriously" then the article is true. 
There is, however, a second sense in which it is true to say 
that a priest in mortal sin can absolve, that is, in merely know: 
ing that the sinner is truly penitent, and in declaring him 
absolved of his sin before God. For God makes His sun to 
shine on the just and the unjust alike. But the pope now, as- 
serts Hus, sending his bulls of indulgence broadcast throughout 
the country, cannot possibly have even this kind of power, for 
he cannot know whether the recipients living throughout christ 
endom are truly contrite and thus absolved before God, being 
all the time at Rome. In this second sense, Hus admits, the 
article is wrong. And here we have an interesting personal 
touch. Having denied that he has taught the article in this 
sense, he asserts that they cannot prove that he has ever thus 
taught, unless, he adds, their proof be effected by the exercise 
of secular power, and before a pilatine court, saying against 
the innocent: We have a law, and by this law he should die. 
(f 239b) He then bewails the fact that many so-called Christians 
seem to be satisfied with the worthless absolution received in 


INTRODUCTION. XXI 


these bulls. “It is clear that, (f 240a) disregarding the law of 
Christ,... they blindly condemn these articles and brazenly 
. pronounce them condemned, a procedure without justification 
in the inviolable law of Christ, making capital out of their 
titles of Magister and Doctor." 

| | The third article, Hus points out, is published from the 
pulpits (in ambonibus) “against us" as erroneous: A good 
and discreet priest, although not a curate or anyone appointed 
to a particular parish, can absolve the parishioner of another 
parish (parrochianum alienum) without leave of his own 
priest (who may be) less good and discreet. Hus defines l1- 
cencia in such a way as to show that licence from unfit su- 
periors is really no licence at all, and the absence of it no 
hindrance to receiving permission direct from God. Tercia 
est licencia absolvendi solum divina, spiritualis 
et invisibilis sine concursu alicuius pretensi sue 
perioris prelatisymoniaci,eoquod Christus quod: 
cunque potest operare in penitente per aliquem 
alium hominem aliquando. (f 241a). Hus's conclusion is 
that Christians have a right to those things that are necessary 
to their salvation, and when they cannot receive them from 
their own priests, they are justified in receiving from good 
priests anywhere the word of God, spiritual advice and the 
sacraments. Human traditions are certainly not to be honored 
above the ordinances of Christ. 

l| The fourth article: that the pope and other prelates of the 
chuch and ecclesiastics should not possess temporal goods. 
(f 242b) After defining ownership of temporal possessions as 
the right of acquisition and permanent disposal, he goes on to 
show the unscriptural nature of that sort of possession by those 
who claim to be the successors of the apostles. It is furthermore 
from a practical point of view inexpedient — distractivus 
plus et impeditivus — to the true and fruitful ministry 
of the gospel of Christ. The argument is supported by a con: 
siderable quotation from Bernard's letters to Pope Eugenius 
which Hus often used. Hus's own argument on this fourth 
article is quite brief. The body of the argument is furnished 
by Jacobellus *'posicio", (ff 243b—253b) which is largely made 
up of quotations from Jerome, Augustine, Chrysostom and 
Gregory. As mentioned above, Hus does take some liberties 


wma 


XXII INTRODUCTION. 


with Jacobellus's text, interpolating and abbreviating in such a 
way as to give us reason for thinking that it was a work of 


.collaboration in the first place. To this edited "posicio" Hus 


adds a fourfold classification of the status of the clergy with 
regard to temporalities. The first, instituted by Christ, is that 
in which the clergy received the necessities of life from the 
laity in return for spiritual alms; the second, laudable, yet less 
well founded in Scripture, in which the clergy received tithes 
and offerings as in the Old Testament; the third state of the 
cergy was that formerly in vogue, under the conditions of 
which they received from rulers and rich laity superabundant 
wealth under the title of alms, with the idea that they should 
act as custodians of that wealth and distribute it to the needy 
— the dangers of which are quite evident, though many good 
priests have done much good under it; the fourth state, that 
of Hus's own day, is absolutely unwarranted and antiscriptu- 
ral when the clergy seek worldly dominion and wealth for 
their own use. This fourth state is truly an "abomination of 
desolation’. 

The fifth article, “To assert that the indulgences which 
the pope and other prelates of the church give according to the 
definition and statutes of the church are worthless, and that the 
faithful are deceived by them, is erroneous and schismatic", 
Hus defends rather briefly (ff 254a— 256a) with arguments prez 
viously used in his own tract Adversus Indulgencias Papales’’. 
(Op. I, 175 sqq). He defines indulgence quite conventionally as 
free remission of guilt which the guilty one should bear for 
his crime — which indulgence is a perquisite of God alone, 
just as the forgiveness of sin is from God alone. Granted that 
remission and indulgence appertain only to God, there is, ne 
vertheless, in the church, an instrumental indulgence, belonging 
to the office of priest and pastor, which the apostles exempli: 
fied for their successors, an integral part of the ministration of 
the gospel. But this is not the way in which the maliciously 
ignorant popes and prelates of the church have given indulgenz 
ces. For they have been incapacitated by their evil and selfish 
lives from administering a gospel of grace and love. Rather 
do they labor for Satan and have the keys of hell than of 
heaven, very Antichrists persecuting the faithful. (f 236a) There 
is a digression at this point in which Hus discusses the sort of 


INTRODUCTION. XXIII 


power that Christ desired to be passed on to his disciples, 
the argument revolving, naturally enough, around the church 
doctrine of the potestas clavium. Christ, says Hus, pro- 
vided for the administration of certain sacraments by the apo 
stles and, by inference, by their successors, *which He insti- 
tuted for the eternal salvation of the human race’. The sacraz 
ment of penance is one of these thus instituted by which 
"actual mortal sin and venial.sin is done away". The ad: 
ministration of this as of the other sacraments is the work of 
priests and presbyters, the true successors of Christ’s apostles. 
The power of judging whether a sinner is truly repentant and 
consequently worthy or unworthy of readmission into the body 
of the church constitutes the authority of the keys. Hus, fol 
lowing the Lombard, quotes Augustine as an authority for this 
definition. Having thus connected the question of the power 
of the keys with the sacrament of penance, Hus proceeds to 
discuss the implications of the practice of indulgences from a 
more theoretical point of view. The rest of this article (ff 257*— 
262”) is very much in the manner of his own commentary on 
the relevant sections in the IV Sent. of the Lombard. And it 
may be remarked in passing that this section in our tractate 1s 
more distinctly academic and moderate in tone than much of 
his writing during this period. The priest, of himself, Hus ar 
gues, can hardly know the degree of penitence in any given 
case, for God seldom gives that sort of revelation in our day. 
"It is enough as a general thing for the priest to tell the sinner 
how repentant, in a given case, he should be" (f 259*) and what 
suffering he should be willing to endure. For "no priest, of 
whatsoever grade, can know beyond peradventure concerning 
those who are truly penitent, exactly how much they must do 
(merentur) in order that the due punishment (pena) for 
their sins may be remitted, in this world or in purgatory, since 
they have no revelation on this subject. And consequently the 
indulgences from punishment and guilt now, falsely indeed, 
current, are unreasonable (non sunt in rerum natura), 
are not based on scripture, and are therefore to be rejected by 
the faithful, and contemned as errors and heresies sowed by the 
father of lies". (f 259°) But there is one who can grant remise 
sion from punishment and guilt — the great high priest, Jesus 
Christ. His true vicars, conforming themselves to His lite and 


XXIV INTRODUCTION. 


precepts, have the two keys of power and knowledge, by which 
the penitent is proclaimed bound or loosed by God. The pos: 
session of the key of knowledge is conditioned on a deep 
knowledge of the Old and New Laws, and the prescription of 
the proper remedy for sin is not to be found in monetary fines, 
but in devout study and meditation on the divine law. Fur- 
thermore these two keys were more abundantly possessed and 
exercised by Paul than by Peter for the richness of his ministry. 
Their followers should ponder the significance of that super- 
iority. : 

The sixth article: If the pope or his vicegerents or other 
prelates of the church, with jurisdiction over their subjects, should 
excommunicate anyone denounced, accused, cited and contuma- 
cious — to assert that that excommunication is senseless (frie 
vola) or surreptitious and consequently void, is not true, but 
rather contrary to law. (f 262°) He refers immediately to the 
excommunication pronounced upon himself by Colonna in Feb. 
1411.* (Doc. ed. Palacky, 202) in a way that has many points 
of similarity in tone and argument with his letter to John Barbat 
of May (?) 1411. (Doc. 16—18; Novotny, Kor. a Dokumenty, 
89—92; Op. I 94—95) He quotes the Lombard's conclusions 
on the justification for excommunication as he does later in 
the “De Ecclesia" (cap. X), pointing out that one of the pur- 
poses of excommunication is to prevent other Christians from 
becoming infected by the errors of one of their number — 
a sort of spiritual quarantine — but he stresses the necessity 
for a faithful examination of the delict or error, such examin- 
ation to be demanded, if need be, by the civil power. He sug: 
gests too that no individual prelate should take it upon himself 
to judge in such cases, but that the universitas fidelium 
in communitate illa or a superior named by that com: 
munity should act as judge. This section (ff 265*— 264^) dealing

with the theory of coactive procedure in trials of those who 
are "cited" is extremely interesting in its ecclesiastico-political 
implications. He even refers to rule by the abler party in the 
college of priests — aut sanioris partis illius (collegii 
sacerdotum). (f 264^?) He finds in Paul's use of excommu: 
nication a warrant for this community judgment when the apostle 
says, "vobis congregatis in meo spiritu cum virtute 
Ihesu Christi tradere huiusmodi sathane'". (f 264^) 
By this emphasis on the participation of the whole Christian 
community or its duly chosen representatives in the act of ex- 
communication Hus hopes to protect the state and the church 
from unjust penalties inflicted by selfish or pharisaical prelates, 
a procedure too often known in his day. This conclusion he 
substantiates from the Lombard and Scripture, drawing atten- 
tion to the softer side of Christ's message. “For this Christ cal- 
led Himself a physician, not a ruler, for it is said, John 5, that 
God did not send His son into the world to judge the world, 
but that the world through Him should be saved." (f 267°) 
Since excommunication is separation from or deprivation of 
communion, Hus takes up a discussion of three kinds of com: 
munion. The first mode of communion is the communio 
sanctorum mentioned in the Apostles’ Creed, an article whose 
truth depends on no pope or prelate, but on Christ Himself. 
The second kind is implied in participation in the sacrament 
of the Eucharist which, when worthily taken, leads to come 
munion of the first kind. Communion in the third sense is the 
congregation of all those who believe in Christ, of whatsoever 
degree of faith and Christian perfection, including even the 
wicked. This, he adds, is the commonly accepted use of the 
word communio. (f 269^) It is from this more inclusive com- 
munion that the popes and prelates so freely and often unjustly 
cut men off by their bulls of excommunication. But, argues 
Hus, anyone worthy of participation in the first kind of com: 
munion (spiritual) must be allowed to come to the Lord's table, 
and join in communion in the second sense (sacramental). This 
worthiness is measurable simply enough according to Christ's 
standards (f271*). But surely anyone worthy of spiritual com 
munion and consequently of sacramental union with the body 
of Christ is not to be thought unworthy of membership in the 
third and larger political society, which already admittedly con: 


XXVI INTRODUCTION. 


tains many wicked. Conversely, none who is unworthy of the 
third sort of communion should dare to participate in the sa- 
crament of the,Lord’s body and blood, for he then would eat 
and drink to his own condemnation. That is to say that Hus 
makes the spiritual worthiness of a Christian the test of all 
membership in the church of Christ. Rejected by the standards 
of Christ's law as unworthy, no "viator" has the right to join 
the other subordinate classes. 
omnis christianus malus, qui abscisus est a commu: 
nione primo modo dicta per peccatum mortale tam:

Hus attributes the great evils that the church has suffered 
through the ages, and particularly in more recent times, of de 
vout Christíans unjustly punished and of wicked men in high 
places in the church with power to excommunicate whom they 
will, to the perversion of these simple scripture truths. These 
pages are extremely bitter in their denunciation of the immo: 
rality of the ruling class of the hierarchy, and are quite in 
Hus' strongest vein, plentifully strengthened by adroitly used 
passages from Scripture and Augustine. When he has finished 
his own defence of the article he subtends Magister Frederick 
Epinge's defence of article 11 of the 45 Wyclif articles: Nul: 
lus prelatus debet aliquem excommunicare, nisi

excommunicat, fit hereticus ex hoc et excommuni 
catus. Hus explains that he is giving this “posicio” especially 
for the benefit of those who are unable to obtain the necessary 
books. This *posicio" at the very beginning posits the three 
kinds of communion which Hus has just defined so carefully, 
and thence he proceeds to an ordered study of the legalities 
involved, arguing from the standpoint of one well versed in 
Canon Law. He marshals his arguments under eight heads, as 
follows : 
1. Distinction between the various kinds of excommunica- 
tion (as Hus above). 
2. The care with which a judge has to proceed in arriving 
at a decision to publish the excommunication. 


INTRODUCTION. XXVII 


5. The purposes for which it may be published. 

4. A proof that there should be no excommunication unless 
for mortal sin, and even then not properly, unless for 
contumacy. 

5. Further proof that none should be excommunicated even 
in cases of contumacy, unless he be known to be exz 
communicate of God (v. arg. 1). 

6. Discussion of power in general as given to those in 
authority. 

7. Extrinsic authority can not possibly harm the just, as far 
as his righteousness is concerned. 

8. How and by whom and how far an unjust sentence is 
to be feared. 

These eight points are all proven or expounded with the aid 
of arguments and copious citations aptly chosen from Canon 
Law, Scripture and the fathers. His conclusions are of course 
essentially those of Hus. 

The seventh article (f 292*) reads: To assert that the pope 
cannot absolve for just cause or from upright motives from 
punishment and guilt one who is contrite and has confessed, 
when the opposite seems true, is schismatical. 

This subject has been partially treated in the fifth article 
(Indulgences), but Hus here takes up the papal office itself, 
its rise, and the mischief attendant upon its present exalted 
and unscriptural position. The system of papal election, he as- 
serts, is farcical, allowing three ‘vicars of Christ" at one time. 
The fact that a majority of Christendom assents to an election 
means nothing, as he shows by referring (f 294*) to the position 
of Elias who remained the sole guardian of truth in his day, 
and, in later times, of Hilary of Poitiers and Athanasius, who 
alone kept the true faith. For, concludes Hus, the election of 
men does not of itself suffice to guarantee as an article of faith 
that this (or that) pope, living thus (in sin) is in the sight of 
God the true pope or bishop. But rather did Christ give the 
gospel rule, “By their fruits shall ye know them,” whereby we 
‘can know if this or that one is such and so great, sent by God. 
He devotes some pages to a restatement of the argument, well 
known in his day (Ockham, Marsilio and Wyclif used it often) 
that Paul was certainly not inferior to Peter, had publicly re- 
buked him, and that none of the other eleven apostles received 


XXVIII INTRODUCTION. 


orders from Peter, or that Peter had ever claimed such authority. 
He treats the subject from many angles, showing from Script. 
ure and the fathers that Peter's jurisdiction should have been 
at Antioch rather than at Rome, that Constantine had no right 
to give temporal power to any bishop, that the successors of 
the other apostles have equal power with those of Peter. He 
then returns to the subject matter of the early part of the pre 
ceding chapter and shows “that neither the pope nor other 
bishops or prelates, in so far as they are true successors of the 
apostles, should have coactive jurisdiction over others". He 
points out that Christ never compelled anyone to the observance 
of His law in this world, and His followers are not taught to 
compel anyone to a like observance. In this respect popes and 
prelates using the civil power to enforce spiritual discipline are 
disobedient to Christ's command and example. The law of 
Scripture does not easily lend itself to the sanctioning of the 
exercise of temporal power either directly or indirectly. “For 
the gospel law is given that we may be directed concerning 
those things that are of eternal salvation to be fulfilled by men, 
and rejected only with fearful consequences (miserie), in 
which (direction) it is indeed sufficient and perfect." (f 305*.) 
Thus the earnest priest, filled with the spirit of Jesus, should 
judge all things according to the spirit of the Master, and pro: 
claim to others the things that are to be understood. And he 
concludes his defence of the seventh article (for the argument 
seems to be complete) with a short paragraph on the obliga- 
tions laid upon secular rulers of keeping the clergy free from 
the sins of wealth and worldly power, of maintaining the prie 
mitive purity of the Church. 
...et primevam Christi ordinacionem pro viribus 
introducant. (f 3067.) 


Sources. 


During the last forty odd years particularly, Hus' works 
have been painstakingly examined with a view to finding the 
source or sources of his teachings. There has been a great deal 
of controversy over his alleged indebtedness to Wyclif. Much 
has been proven and much more alleged without sufficient 
proof, but it may be accepted as fairly sure that Hus often 


INTRODUCTION. XXIX 


| used Wyclifs words, as much perhaps as a page at a time, 
without express acknowledgement. It has thus far been over: 
looked, with several rare exceptions, that at the time this was 
certainly not considered unethical. Many of Wyclif’s works 
were well known in Prague from the last decade of the 14" 
century, and later there were numbers of students in the Uni- 
versity that were as familiar with Wyclif’s writings as Hus 
himself. He could hardly under these circumstances have been 
intending to use another man’s learning in such a way as to 
pass it off for his own — when many of his hearers knew 
Wyclif as well as he did. A good deal that has been said on 
the subject, both in Czechoslovakia and in other countries, has 
clearly disregarded this concomitant of the conditions of literary 
production in Hus’ day. One might as well accuse Hus of 
plagiarizing the Lombard or Thomas Aquinas, when he uses 
their definitions in his lectures without acknowledging them as 
the authors, as accuse him of plagiarizing, in our sense of the 
word, from Wyclif, a recognized Doctor of Oxford, whose 
writings, by that very fact, became virtually public property. 
Wyclif himself had borrowed much without acknowledgment 
from Guillermus Peraldus as well as from Grosseteste*. Nor 
must we forget that a large part of the literary output of Hus 
as well as of Wyclif was in no small degree pedagogic in 
purpose. They were both university lecturers "by trade", and 
alarge part of their task was to present current thought, both 
theological and philosophical, to their students. 

While the last word has not been said, and will doubtless 
not be said for many years to come on this moot question of 
Hus’ dependence on Wyclif, yet it is safe to say that the final 
verdict will not so much emphasize the verbal parallels to be 
found in the works of the two reformers as the parallel pre: 
ceding and accompanying conditions under which the two men 
lived and worked. Meantime the sincere student is hardly war 
ranted in taking his convictions at secondzhand from quarters 
unfavorably disposed toward the Slavic race in general. The ulti: 
mate value of, for instance, Prof. Loserth's undoubtedly schol: 
arly works in this field is greatly prejudiced by his too evident 


oe 


* Cf. Dr. Loserth’s brief but illuminating studies of Wyclif's dependence 
upon these two scholars, Johann von Wiclif und Gulielmus Peraldus, Wien 
1916; Johann von Wiclif und Robert Grosseteste; Wien, 1918. 


XXX INTRODUCTION. 


predisposition to make Hus get everything, even his Bible 
quotations, from Wyclif. Even in this fruitful field of schol 
arship, it is possible to prove too much. 

The footnotes in the text indicate, albeit not completely, 
yet enough to show his methods, what sources Hus used in 
the composition of this tractate. He undoubtedly had. before 
him the Bible, with the Glossa Ordinaria" of Walafrid Strabo 
and the “Glossa Interlinearis" of Anselm of Laon, the Canon 
Law, the Lombard’s IV Sententiarum, Wyclif's "De Ecclesia,” 
"De Potestate Pape" and several minor tracts and Matthew 
of Janov's "Regulae". It is possible that he also used codices 
containing works of the early fathers, Augustine, Chrysostom, 
Jerome and Ambrose, but almost all the quotations he gives 
from these writers are to be found in one of the previously 
mentioned textebooks. In several cases, as e. g. when Hus quotes 
the Lombard's version of some church father, a virtual paraphrase 
of the original, the mediate use of authorities is perfectly clear. 
At other times it is hard to decide whether he has used his 
sources directly or not. He makes occasional mistakes in quot: 
ation, but where these are not so considerable as to change 
the sense, nothing has been said concerning them. There is one 
quotation, from Aristotle’s "Politica" (f 504^) which has given 
me no little concern. I have been unable to find whence he 
could have gotten it unless from a direct use of Aristotle. Per: 
haps someone better acquainted with mediaeval bibliography 
than I can fill that gap. 

Here as elsewhere Hus has made great use of the Lombard.* 
The recent appearance of the IV" Book of the “Regulae Vet. 
et Novi Test." of Matthew of Janov, ed. Dr. Odlozilík, 1926, 
makes it possible to ascertain that, contrary to current belief, 
Hus knew and used Matthew's work extensively. Almost his 
entire treatment of excommunication (this ed pp. 853—102) pres 
ceding the "posicio" of Epinge, is taken bodily from the IV" 
Book of the “‘Regulae” (ed. Odlozilik, pp. 30—50). There may 
be more such cases of his use of his Czech predecessors, but as this 
Introduction is already in the press, I can mention only this one. 


* Cf. Loserth, Hus und Wiclif, 2nd ed. 1925, p. 189. Hus' distinctions 
between Sacramentum et res etc. are taken directly from the Lombard, IV, 
dist. VIII, and it is perfect nonsense to attribute them to Wyclif.as Lo: 
serth does. 


INTRODUCTION. XXXI 


The one reference to Armacanus (f 2987), is, of course, taken 
directly from Wyclif’s *Ad Argumenta Emuli Veritatis". Fitz- 
Ralph’s works were probably inaccessible to Hus, and to have 
taken such a quotation from another man's book was as legi- 
timate as quoting Augustine from the Lombard or Canon Law. 
There was no other way. We find sections in this tractate that 
reproduce what he had previously said in the “Tractatus ad: 
versus Indulgencias Papales" and the "actus" mentioned above, 
“De Decimis", as well as arguments which he uses later, gene: 
rally worked over, in the “De Ecclesia", *De Sex Erroribus", 
"Contra Palec&", *Ad Scripta M. Stanislai de Znoimo". These 
cross-references have, as far as possible, been pointed out in 
the footnotes, but there will doubtless be found others, con- 
firming, it is believed, the dating even more surely. 

The interpolation of the two "posiciones" of Jacobellus and 
Epinge brings out several interesting facts. In the first case, no 
mention is made of Jacobellus, the reputed author. The reason is 
clear. Hus probably was cozauthor (Sedlák, Studie a Texty, II, 187), 
and takes further liberties with the text, abridging and amend» 
ing where he thinks fit to answer the needs of the present tract. In 
that case it would hardly be necessary to proclaim the partial aut: 
horship of a living cozworker, when their collaboration was pro: 
bably a fact of common knowledge. In the second case, however, 
he does mention Epinge as the author, because he gives the tract 
exactly as Epinge wrote it, neither adding nor abridging, although 
it is probable that there was collaboration here as well as in the 
case of Jacobellus. On the last few pages of the “‘posicio’’, (f 289° to 
the end) there is a whole series of quotations that are reproduced 
in Hus's De Sex Erroribus". Whether Hus appropriated from 
Epinge, or helped the younger man in the first place in arranging 
his authorities would seem a fruitless question, but the large pro: 
portion of Scripture quotations would seem to point to Hus's guiz 
dance. In any case it suggests the close relationships existing be: 
tween Hus, the recognized leader of the reform party, and his 
subordinates. 


Literature. 


The first notice of this unfinished tractate was that made by Dr. 
Flajshans, as mentioned above, under the erroneous title “Against 
the Rokyzcanians" (Proti Rokycanskym). Dr. Flajshans thought at 


c 


XXXII INTRODUCTION. 


the time that it should be dated early in 1413. He later published 
a Czech translation of about one half the tractate (ff 227*— 269?) 
in “Collected Works of John Hus” (Sebrané Spisy Husovy) I, 
333 sqq. Prof. Jan Sedlak some years later corrected the error in 
the title (Studie a Texty, II, 196, 1914) and in his “M. Jan Hus" 
(1915) p. 252, note 3, spoke of the tractate as “his first unfinished 
defence against the doctors", showing further that the tractate 
should be dated late in 1412, and pointing out that Jacobellus's 
"posicio" had been incorporated into the tractate. Prof. Sedlák 
had not, however, noticed the great liberties that Hus had taken 
with the original “posicio” of his friend, but did put forward the 
suggestion which we have adopted that this is a case of collabora: 
tion (Studie a Texty, II, p. 195 —6). Several years thereafter Prof. 
Novotny, without having been able to use the manuscript gave 
a very complete résumé of the work in his ''M. Jan Hus, Zivot 
a Uéent’ (Life and Teaching of Master John Hus) I, 2, pp. 161 —2, 
dating it more exactly than either of the preceding scholars, and 
adducing conclusive evidence making sure the hypothesis upon 
which the conjecture on page XVII is based, viz: that he had 
friends in the monastery at Rokycany who would gladly have 
brought him a copy of these 16 charges. (Op. cit. p. 162, note 1.) 

The catalogue of the works of Jacobellus recently published 
(1925) by Dr. F. M. Barto$ mentions our tractate as containing 
the “‘posicio” of Jacobellus. Nowhere, however, has it been pointe 
ed out that the alterations made by Hus in Jacobellus's original 
"posicio" were as considerable as we find them to be, or that 
Epinge's *posicio'* has been here reproduced in toto. 


The Edition. 


This edition, with the introduction in Czech, forms N? 15 
of “Sbirka pramenü nábozenského hnutí XIV. a XV. stol." 
published by the Czech Academy of Sciences. Preceding num: 
bers have been 8 fasciculi of Hus' works edited by Dr. Flaj& 
hans, four books of Matthew of Janov's "Regulae Vet. et Nov. 
Testamenti", edited by Dr. Kybal, the fifth book edited by Dr. 
Odlozilík (1926), and Prof. Novotny's Documents and Corresz 
pondence of Hus, 1920. This edition is No. 10. of the works 
of Hus. 

Editing a unique manuscript of any work and that the ori: 
ginal obligates the editor to reproduce everything within the 


INTRODUCTION. XXXIII 


limits of the incipit and the explicit, even to errors. This the 
present editor has striven to do. The punctuation of the ma- 
nuscript has, of course, been disregarded entirely, but the spelling 
has been given as in the original, even when there are various 
spellings used, as e. g. apostoli and appostoli; verum: 
tamen and verumptamen. Several times it has been found 
necessary, in order to avoid confusion, to correct a spelling in 
the text, but the original has always been given in the notes. 
When Xs, Xi has been written, Christus is given in the text, 
but when the scribe has written out Cristus, as sometimes oce 
curs, no change has been made. References to church fathers 
are to Migne's Patrologia, PL, Patrologia Latina, and PGL, 
Patrologia GraecazLatina, by volume and column. Scripture 
quotations within other quotations have been disregarded and 
only very clear indirect scripture references are indicated with 
a Cf. in the footnotes. Those who are familiar with the labour 
involved in editing a work of this sort from the highly ab: 
breviated Latin of the later Middle Ages will, it is hoped, 
exercise the virtue of Christian charity and bear with the 
shortcomings of the edition. The editor is only too conscious 
of his imperfect preparation for a work of this sort, but if 
his'editio princeps’ is reasonably usable, he is more than repaid 
for the several years' labour involved. 

S. Harrison Thomson. 

Prague, Czechoslovakia Dec. 1926.

apostolice vel kathedre Moysi. Ideo bene dixit, quod illi, qui 
sedent in kathedra, hoc est secundum illam racionem, qua sic 
sedent et innituntur dono et officio promulgandi veraciter legem 
dei, tunc sic quecunque dicunt, facite, quia non nisi 
veritatem legis dicunt. Sed post additur: secundum autem 
opera eorum nolite facere, quia pravam vitam ducunt. 
Et sic sunt sine gracia in peccato mortali existentes, quamvis 
ceteris utilem veritatem dicant. Et sic Scribe et Pharisei possunt 
habere a deo donum spiritus sancti, officium sive potestatem 
ordinis ad edificandum non se, sed alios. Sufficiencia ergo to: 
talis talis doni et officii spiritualis est plenitudo spiritualis, que 
in se est invisibilis, que non exterius in corpore, sed intus in 
interiori homine infusa subiectatur, que hic a nobis non potest 
cognosci, nisi a posteriori debiliter et probabiliter noticia, quia 
est ex propriis operacionibus sive actibus illius officii sive doni 
extra sensibiliter ad proximos sive eorum edificacionem emanan- 
tibus. Habent enim talia dona supernaturalia, gratis data et non 
ex se gratum faciencia, proprias operaciones, exterius sensibiles 
ad edificacionem proximorum, quamvis talia dona habentes sunt 
in peccato mortali * sine gracia gratum faciente. Ex quibus patet, 
quod in papa vel quibuscunque episcopis sive prelatis symonia: 
cis, cupidis, concubinariis, ignaris in lege dei, salutem anima: 
rum negligentibus, neque actus proprios sacerdocii Christi ex 
ercentibus, non est de substancia fidei, quod fideles credant 
in eis esse datum a deo illud donum supermundanum. Si enim 
fideles considerarent in eis proprios actus sensibiles illius doni 
ad edificacionem animarum, tunc iuxta ewangelicam regulam 
deberent operibus credere, et ex illis operibus iudicare eos 
esse in tali officio a Christo constitutos, eciam si existerent

prophecie fuit concessum Balaam gentili et iniquo pontifici Cay:

obligant nos in novo testamento, quia Christus, qui venit non! 
solvere legem, sed implere, cuius factum est * interpres, quomodo 
vetus testamentum debet intelligi et servari; docuit enim, quo 
modo ipse et sui apostoli et eorum vicarii deberent vivere pau 
perem vitam exproprietarie, quod duravit per trecentos annos 
et amplius usque ad dotacionem Cesaris Constantini, ad cuius 
dotacionis crementum proporcionaliter decrevit et valde defecit 
possessionata multitudo secularium dominorum et hoc in vir 
tute et potencia quoad suum officium. 

Item omnia moralia iudicialium et ceremonialium veteris 
testamenti sunt observanda hodie. Cum ergo conversacio pauper 
et exproprietaria sit moralis, videtur, quod post Christum pau: 
perem et apostolos pauperes, maxime nobis in exemplum da 
tos, videtur, quod talis conversacio pauper specialiter a clero 
debeat observari. Sicut enim nunc mater ecclesia ad finem ses 
culi est senior, sic debet esse specialiter in clero religiosior et 
per consequens deserendo mundum sponso Christo propinquior. 
Sed heu! in hoc clerus maxime retrocedit, igitur etc. Item de 
cimas et illa, que sonant in lucrum, nos clerici accipimus, non 
expressa auctoritate legis nove, sed tantum veteris. Videtur 
ergo, quod magis religiosum sit temporalibus abdicare et sez 
cundum ewangelicam pauperem vitam clericis vivere, quod est 
in ewangelio expressatum, quam decimas tollere sine expressa 
auctoritate ewangelica. Nam constat ex facto Christi, quod uti- 
que minus religiosum est congregare decimas et oblaciones quam 
vivere exproprietarie vita paupere ewangelica, non congregando 
decimas et oblaciones etc. 

Sed si contra supradicta obicitur per illud I* ad Thimotheum 
3° et ad Titum 2*: Oportet? episcopum esse hospita: 
lem, quod non bene videtur posse fieri sine dotacione tempo 
ralium, igitur etc, ad hoc potest responderi, quod oportere 
episcopum hospitalem esse", duobus modis potest intelligi: uno 
modo necessitate simpliciter et absolute, alio modo ex condi 
cione. Rursum episcopum esse hospitalem eciam potest duobus 
modis intelligi: vel quantum ad effectum et firmum propositum 
solum, vel quantum ad affectum et exteriorem effectum. Si ergo 
ly oportet intelligatur necessitate absolute simpliciter suo modo, 
hospitalis vero dum notat hospitalitatem quoad efectum princi-

paliter et propositum, tunc verum * est secundum Apostolum,

exterius, ut sit sensus, quod oportet episcopum non simpliciter 
et absolute, sed ex condicione, ut si, quando habuerit, loco et 
tempore oportunis secundum posse hospitalitatem miseris in: 
digentibus exhibeat. Et ad illum sensum iterum dictum Apostoli 
est verum, quod oportet episcopum esse hospitalem, hoc est, si 
et in quantum et quando* occurreret a casu et ex improviso 
habere temporalia, quod de eis tunc exigere deberet hospitaliz 
tatem indigentibus. Intelligendo autem ly oportet pro necessiz 
tate absoluta simpliciter et absolute suo modo et in suo ge 
nere, et hospitalitatem intelligendo quoad suum effectum, sic 
quod simpliciter et absolute et ad semper necessario require: 
retur, quod episcopus esset in efectu exterius hospitalis in tem 
poralibus, sic non est ad illum sensum dictum Apostoli verum, 
nec sic debet intelligi. Non enim oportet episcopum habere 
temporalia superhabundanter et possessionate atque seculariter 
ea sibi conservare atque eadem sibi vendicare, contenciose eis 
intendere atque talibus dominari. Nam summus pontifex Chris- 
tus secundum exteriorem effectum ut communiter hospitalita-

ut supra allegatum est. Ex quibus patere potest, quod iuxta 
merita penitencium non plus de culpa vel pena relaxare potest 
Romanus episcopus, quam alter quicunque sacerdos. Secundo 
patet, quod non plus vel minus potest prelatus, episcopus vel 
papa vel quicunque alius indulgere a pena vel culpa ministe- 
rialiter et vicarie, nisi quantum penitens meruit secundum suam 
capacitatem. Cum ergo communiter papa et prelati nesciant com 
muniter distincte merita christianorum, patet, quod frivole proz 
nuncciant eis et presumptuose * ex luciferina superbia, nullius 
cognoscentes meritum adequatum neque capacitatem neque super 
hoc dei revelacionem habentes. Et patet error modernorum pre: 
latorum, qui sub specie pietatis questum querentes, seducunt 
populum christianum per indulgencias et falsas promissiones 
a spiritu cupiditatis et mendacii et non a spiritu veritatis adin- 
ventas. Et patet, quam periculosa sunt iam tempora Antichristi, 
in quibus populus christianus non docetur de lege dei, quomodo 
debet penitere per artam viam ducentem ad celum, sed peri: 
culosissime assecuratur, beatificatur populus, quod sit absolutus 
a peccatis; et non solum a culpa, sed a pena, non solum a prez 
senti pena, sed a futura, quod periculum multi non cognoscunt, 
nisi in exitu mortis; cui populo dicitur a domino per prophetam 
Ysaiam: Popule! meus, qui te dicunt beatum, ipsi 
te decipiunt. 

Et licet iam aliqualiter dictum sit de clavibus ecclesie, que 
per Christi sacerdotes exercentur, tamen adhuc pro clariori inz 
tellectu de eis est amplius disserendum. Notum enim debet 
esse^ et pro fide haberi, quod solus summus pontifex futurorum 
bonorum, virtute increate et benedicte trinitatis verus deus et 
homo, culpam et debitum eterne dampnacionis vere penitenti 
remittit: aliquando priusquam sacerdoti confitetur, aliquando 
tunc, quando confitetur, aliquando post confessionem, quoniam 
ipse est solus agnus dei, qui tollit peccata mundi, qui non 
potest ignorare, quibus remittendum sit peccatum, qui eciam 
affeccione perversa neque iudicat neque errat, quemquam iniuste 
condempnando. Non autem sic ecclesia militans et a forciori, 
non sic papa, episcopus vel quicunque sacerdos. Nam ecclesia 
fallit et fallitur", et quilibet episcoporum potest errare quando- 
que vel inclinari affeccione perversa, vel utroque modo ab ewan:

nari contra fideles zelatores et promulgatores ewangelice veritatis, 
que’ a fidelibus debet respui tanquam machinamentum iniquum 
et dolosum Antichristi. Prima communio est dicta principaliter 
et proprie et est solum bonorum et iustorum. Communio? secundo 
modo accepta dicitur proprie ad efectum, quem facit, sed non 
principaliter, nisi solum ob racionem prime, et est malorum et 
bonorum, quantum ad sacramentalem manducacionem. Come 
munio tercio modo accepta est dicta neque principaliter neque 
proprie, quia boni christiani cum malis non habent unitatem 
vite et spiritus in Christo, sicut nec vivum membrum habet 
unitatem cum mortuo, sicut nec potest esse communicacio Christi 
cum Belial, nec lumen potest habere societatem cum tenebris. 
Unde Apostolus: Non! potestis calicem Christi bi 
bere et calicem demoniorum; et iterum: Que est con 
vencio Christi ad Belial, aut que participacio 
lucis ad tenebras? Sed solum huiusmodi communio tercio 
modo accepta est bonorum christianorum et malorum dumtaxat 
in actu credendi et in conversacione mutua et in ceremoniis 
et in adinvencionibus et tradicionibus seniorum et in sensibili 
communicacione sacramentorum. 

Ex quibus iam dictis sequitur primo, quod omnis christia: 
nus, qui est particeps communionis primo modo accepte, est 
dignus ad communionem secundo modo dictam, et expedit sibi 
ac utile est, ut sepe se disponat ad communionem sacramen- 
talem secundo modo dictam. Patet hoc, quia omnis talis, exi- 
stens vivum et verum membrum ecclesie Christi et eius come 
munionis, iam manducat corpus Christi spiritualiter per fidem, 
spem et caritatem. Sed omnis talis spiritualiter manducans poz 
test et expedit sibi sepe corporis sacramentum Christi mandu

Item quia est una vita!, unus spiritus, una fides, unus panis, unus

Secundo sequitur: cuiuslibet manducantis corpus Christi sunt 
omnes alii in ecclesia participes. Patet hoc iterum ex predictis, 
quia digna manducacio sacramenti est factum simpliciter bonum, 
quod si alicui est malum, hoc est ex accidenti et secundum quid,

eligat alias voluntate actuali neque in verbo exprimat, tamen 
ita est de facto, ita quod non tantum pro se sumit, sed pro aliis, 
ut declaratum est; ymno tenetur hoc velle voluntate saltim habi: 
tuali. Item sequitur, quod persone devote laicales utriusque sez

bus et in spiritu profectum, sicut et boni sacerdotes faciunt 
consolacionem plebibus Christi, multas sepe missas celebrantes.

in bonis penitentibus cuiuscunque sexus * laicalis debet quis: 
quam scandalisari, sed magis spiritualiter edificari.

tuas assecuraciones, per fictas indulgencias et per mendosas

acceptam et fit de societate sanctorum, qui et litigare non‘

credencium in Christum et fide formata et fide informi, bono 
rum christianorum simul et malorum. Et hec est wlgaris accepcio 
communionis et minus propria. Primo modo communio dicta 
est principaliter et proprie, et est solorum bonorum. Secundo 
modo proprie dicitur ab efectu, quem facit, sed non principa: 
liter, nisi solum ob racionem prime, et est malorum et bonorum 
quantum ad sacramentalem manducacionem. * Tercio modo 
accepta est dicta neque principaliter neque proprie, quia boni 
christiani cum malis non habent unitatem vite et spiritus in 
Christo, sicut vivum membrum cum mortuis et lumen cum tene

norum spiritualium per sanctos aquisitorum participacio, de qui-

singula membra sana, similiter in arbore, in qua humor per 
radicem attractus dividitur in singulos eius ramos non aridos.

Non enim dixit Christus: Fac tu hoc, Petre, et aliis apostolis 
sic faciendi tribuas potestatem, sed dixit: Facite in plurali 
et omnibus indifferenter. Idem quoque per omnia senciendum 
est de clavium potestate, sive eisdem verbis tradita fuerit apo 
stolis, sive aliis aut alio tempore, ut hiis, que habentur Iohan: 5 
nis 20. Postquam enim Christus dixit apostolis: Sicut! misit 
me pater, et ego mitto vos. Insufflavit et dixit eis: 
Accipite spiritum sanctum. Quorum remiseritis 
peccata, remissa erunt, et quorum retinueritis, re: 
tenta sunt. Dixit ergo Christus: Mitto vos sicut pater 10 
me misit. Nec dixit Petro aut alteri apostolo singulariter: 
Mitto te sicut pater etc. et tu alios mitte. Nec rursum dicitur: 
Christus insufflavit ei, sed dixit eis, non uni per alterum. Nec 
dixit Christus ad Petrum: Accipe spiritum sanctum tu primum ante 
alios omnes et aliis postmodum tribue, sed dixit eis: Accie 15 
pite in plurali et indifferenter omnibus loquens. Et hoc eciam 
est, quod habetur Mt. ultimo, dum dixit Christus ad eos: Eu nz 
tes? ergo docete omnes gentes. Et dixit euntes pluta- 
liter et indifferenter, non dicens Petro: Vade ergo et alios mitte.

exercuit in hoc seculo iudiciariam potestatem coactivam, sed

et quod talem iudiciariam potestatem coactivam cum exercuerit 
in alio seculo*, tunc apostoli cum ipso sedebunt ad iudicandum 
tali iudicio, non ante ea. 

Unde admiracione dignum est valde, cur sibi assumat epis- 
copus seu presbiter aliquis, quicunque sit ille, maiorem aut aliam 
auctoritatem, quam Christus aut eius apostoli habere voluerunt 
in hoc seculo. Ipsi enim in forma servorum iudicati sunt per 
seculi principes, iam vero eorum nostri moderni vocati succes: 
sores presbiteri principibus non solum subesse recusant, contra 
Christi et apostolorum exemplum atque preceptum, verum eciam 
principibus et potestatibus supremis coactiva iurisdicione preesse 
se dicunt, cum tamen? dixit Christus Mt. 10: Ad! reges et 
ad presides ducemini propter me; nec dixit: presides 
aut reges eritis. Et infra subiungit: Non est discipulus 
super magistrum, nec servus super dominum suum.

ARTICULUS VII: DE ABSOLUCIONE PAPALI. 159 


et propterea diversa est temporalium et humanorum actuum 
regula, diversimode dirigens ad hos fines, lex autem divina 
nullomodo? docet contendere aut repetere in iudicio, licet non 
prohibet laicis, ideoque nec de talibus specialia tradit precepta. 
Reliqua vero horum actuum, ut sunt negocia secularia, humana 
lex precipit arceri et transgressores suos puniri. Propter quod 
Luce 12 petenti a Christo humanum iudicium inter se et fratrem 
suum respondit ipse: Homo!, quis me constituit iudi 
cem aut divisorem inter vos? quasi diceret: Non veni 
tale iudicium exercendum; ubi glosa: Non? dignatur esse 
iudex licium nec arbiter facultatum, qui vivorum 
et mortuorum habet iudicium. Non ergo per ewange- 
licam legem comensurari possent sufficienter actus humani? pro 
fine huius seculi, quoniam nec regule talium actuum comensuz 
rative ad proporcionem, quam volunt homines, et licite pro statu 
vite presentis, sunt in ea tradite", sed ab humanis legibus supe 
posite vel tradite vel tradende. Non ergo lex ewangelica est 
imperfecta. Est enim ad hoc data, ut per ipsam dirigamur inme- 
diate de hiis, que sunt eterne salutis, consequende hominibus‘, 
et miserie declinande, in quibus est valde sufficiens et perfecta. 
Non autem data fuit, nec est lex Christi ad civilia litigiosa 
negocia dirimenda propfer finem, quem volunt * homines et 
licite in vita humana. Si enim ex hoc diceretur inperfecta, eque 
convenienter inperfecta dici posset, quam per ipsam medicari 
corporales egritudines vel occeanum navigare nescimus. Et huius 
sentencie testatur glosa* secundum Gregorium super sextum capi: 
tulum prime ad Corinthios, ubi sic ait: Ad? reverenciam 
vestram dico, ut videlicet hii terrenas causas exami 
nent, qui exteriorum rerum sapienciam percepez 
runt; qui autem! spiritualibus donis dotati sunt, 
terrenis non debent negociis implicari; quod si per 
sapienciam rerum exteriorum et terrenarum causarum seu conten: 
cionum intellexisset sacram scripturam, non dixisset: Qui autem 
spiritualibus donis dotati sunt, id est? scriptura sacra, terrenis non 
debent negociis implicari, nec tales secundum has doctrinas invi

cem separasset, amplius, quia talem sapienciam habentes exterio- 
rum rerum prius vocaverat Apostolus et sancti secundum exposicio- 
nem unam contemptibiles in ecclesia, quod tamen nec Apostolus aut 
sancti exponentes eius seriem senserunt de doctis in sacra scriptura. 

Clerici ergo docti a spiritu sancto in lege dei iudicent iudicio 
discrecionis et non coactivo de peccatis inter lepram et lepram 
spiritualem’, de deordinacionibus maximis in ecclesia per omnes 
et singulos status, de abhominacione! desolacionis, iamdudum 
stante in loco sancto', ubi non debet, symoniaca heresi, de con 
cubinatu pseudosacerdotum, de yppocrisi ubique diffusa; iudi- 
cent, inquam, illuminati sacerdotes de hiis nephandissimis malis 
currentibus sic, quemadmodum medicus iudicat urinam esse 


sanam vel egram circa infirmum potentem et divitem, non habens 


super eum iurisdicionem vel iudicium coactivum. Sic spiritualis 
sacerdos, spiritu Ihesu afflatus, omnia diiudicet secundum spi 
ritum Ihesu, et aliis discernenda publicet. Seculares vero domini, 
informati fideliter a sacerdotibus per eorum * spirituale iudicium 
discrecionis, non coactivum, iudicent iudicio coactivo malos obstiz 
natos, nollentes voluntarie converti ad viam veritatis secundum 
sapienciam dei per sanctos sacordotes eis donatam. Et per illud 
coactivum iudicium seculares domini et principes secundum 
huiusmodi regulacionem destruent symoniacam heresim in clero, 
avariciam, concubinatum, superfluas divicias, a debito ministerio 
clericali eos retardantes aufferant caritativa et serpentina pru- 
dencia, et pro honestacione* rei publice et Christi fidelium pau 
perum convertant pseudoprelatos et falsos predicatores, plebanos 
et vicarios scandalosos et seductores de temporalibus, in malicia 
non foveant neque nutriant, sed magis a talibus decimas et census 
aufferendo, pro sacerdotibus fideliter ewangelisantibus populis et 
ministerium sacerdotale pro salute hominum debite explentibus 
ordinent et disponant, et omnem deordinacionem per simulatum 
et symoniacum clerum iam antiquatam eradicent, furta, homicidia, 
latrocinia, adulteria, fornicaciones et omnia crimina patencia' in 
subditis destruant et primevam Christi ordinacionem pro viribus 
introducant.
